\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}}

(21)

of linear functions commutes, meaning that all pathways with the same start and end are the same function.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\big[(\FGamma\circ h^{*})(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big](s_{0},a_{0},\dots,a_{T-1},s_{T})

\displaystyle=\Big[\FGamma\big(h^{*}(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big)\Big](s_{0},a_{0},\dots,a_{T-1},s_{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\big[h^{*}(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big](s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}R_{\mathcal{F}}\big(h(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\big)

\displaystyle=\big[\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big]\big(h(s_{0},a_{0},s_{1}),\dots,h(s_{T-1},a_{T-1},s_{T})\big)

\displaystyle=\big[\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big]\big(h^{T}(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{T-1},a_{T-1},s_{T})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big[h^{T^{*}}\big(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big)\Big](s_{0},a_{0},\dots,a_{T-1},s_{T})

\displaystyle=\big[(h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})(R_{\mathcal{F}})\big](s_{0},a_{0},\dots,a_{T-1},s_{T}).

This shows
\FGamma\circ h^{*}=h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Consequently, the diagram commutes.
∎

The idea will be that the rows of
\bp
,
\bp\circ\FGamma
,
\bp\circ h^{T^{*}}
and
\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*}=\bp\circ h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}
all correspond (via
Proposition

A.1
) to feature beliefs over trajectories, transitions, trajectory abstractions, and transition abstractions, respectively.
We explain this interpretation in detail in
Section

C.3
.
All of these functions “factorize” over trajectories, but of course this need not be the case in reality:
A realistic human could have an intrinsic belief over state transitions, sequences of abstractions, or single abstractions, without this belief “factorizing” in a rational way over state sequences.

Thus, let the following be an extended version of the diagram from
Proposition

C.1
, with new linear functions
\bp^{\prime},\bp^{\prime\prime},\bp^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
This extension is now
not
necessarily commutative anymore:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp^{\prime\prime}}

(22)

To interpret
\bp^{\prime},\bp^{\prime\prime},\bp^{\prime\prime\prime}
on similar grounds as
\bp
, it makes sense to assume that they are row-constant (
Definition

B.1
), but otherwise they can be arbitrary.

C.2
Various human belief models

We take the previous diagrams as the starting point to construct human belief models
Remember that a belief model is of the form
\mathcal{M}=(\Omega,\mathbf{\Lambda},\mathbf{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{V})
.
The sets
\Xi,\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{T}
are four different possible feature sets
\Omega
.

\mathbf{\Lambda}
is given by a composition of linear functions that maps to
\mathds{R}^{\Xi}
.

\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}
is given by a composition mapping to
\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{O}}
.
The space
\mathcal{V}
is either given by the full vector space
\mathds{R}^{\Omega}
, or by images of functions mapping to
\mathds{R}^{\Omega}
.
Overall, using the diagram from
Proposition

C.1
, this leads to the following 9 models, with the superscript denoting the feature space, and the subscript indicating where the valid reward objects “originate from”:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{F},\ \FGamma\circ h^{*},\ \bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S},\ \FGamma,\ \bp\circ\FGamma,\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S},\ \FGamma,\ \bp\circ\FGamma,\ \im(h^{*})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{F}^{T},\ h^{T^{*}},\ \bp\circ h^{T^{*}},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{F}^{T},\ h^{T^{*}},\ \bp\circ h^{T^{*}},\ \im(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\Xi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\Xi,\ \id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}},\ \bp,\ \mathds{R}^{\Xi}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\Xi,\ \id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}},\ \bp,\ \im(\FGamma)\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\Xi,\ \id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}},\ \bp,\ \im(h^{T^{*}})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\Xi,\ \id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}},\ \bp,\ \im(\FGamma\circ h^{*})\big)

For example,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}
is the model from
Example

2.6
;
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the same model, but with valid reward functions restricted to those that only “care about” abstractions;
\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\Xi}
is a model in which the features are given by full trajectories, and there are no restrictions on the valid return functions; etc.

Now,
\bp^{\prime}
naturally gives rise to the following three models, which we color differently to distinguish them more easily:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{F},\ \FGamma\circ h^{*},\ \bp^{\prime}\circ h^{*},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S},\ \FGamma,\ \bp^{\prime},\ \im(h^{*})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S},\ \FGamma,\ \bp^{\prime},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}\big).

Similarly,
\bp^{\prime\prime}
gives rise to the following three models:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{F},\ h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}},\ \bp^{\prime\prime}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{F}^{T},\ h^{T^{*}},\ \bp^{\prime\prime},\ \im(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{F}^{T},\ h^{T^{*}},\ \bp^{\prime\prime},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}\big)

Finally,
\bp^{\prime\prime\prime}
gives rise to a single model:

\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}=\big(\mathcal{F},\ \FGamma\circ h^{*},\ \bp^{\prime\prime\prime},\ \mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\big)

Note that all component linear functions appearing in any of these models (identities,
h^{*},\FGamma,\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}},h^{T^{*}},\bp,\dots,\bp^{\prime\prime\prime}
) are row-constant.
By
Lemma

B.3
then, also all compositions are row-constant, which then implies that all
16
models are balanced, as defined in
Definition

B.2
.
The first
9
models are also faithful (
Definition

2.15
) since all feature belief functions factorize as in
Proposition

2.16
, with
Y
given by
\bp
in all cases.
The other
7
models will typically not be faithful.

C.3
Interpreting the matrix elements

We now interpret the different feature belief functions that appeared in the nine first models of the previous subsection.
Recall that the linear function
\bp:\mathds{R}^{\Xi}\to\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{O}}
“comes from” a function
b:\mathcal{O}\to\Delta(\Xi)\subseteq\mathds{R}^{\Xi}
.
Thus, all matrix elements
\bp_{o\xi}
can be interpreted as a probability
\big[b(o)\big](\xi)
for the trajectory
\xi
when viewing observation
o
.
We now explain similar interpretations for the matrix elements of all the other feature belief functions:

\bp\circ\FGamma
:
It contains matrix elements

(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s,a,s^{\prime})}=\sum_{\xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\delta_{(s,a,s^{\prime})}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1}),

the expected discounted number of times the transition
(s,a,s^{\prime})
is present in the trajectory.

\bp\circ h^{T^{*}}
:
Write
\mathbf{f}
for
(f_{1},\dots,f_{T})
.
Then this contains matrix elements

\displaystyle(\bp\circ h^{T^{*}})_{o\mathbf{f}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)h^{T^{*}}_{\xi\mathbf{f}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\delta_{\mathbf{f}}(h^{T}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\colon h^{T}(\xi)=\mathbf{f}}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)

\displaystyle=\big[b(o)\big]\big((h^{T})^{-1}(\mathbf{f})\big)

\displaystyle=\big[b(o)_{h^{T}}\big](\mathbf{f}).

In the second to last step, we view
b(o)
as a probability distribution that, when evaluated on a set, evaluates to the sum of the probabilities of the set’s elements.
In the last step, we use the definition of the distributional law of a random variable
X
with respect to a probability distribution
P
on the sample space:
P_{X}(x)=P(X^{-1}(x))
.
The result is the believed probability, after observing
o
, of a trajectory with sequence of abstractions
\mathbf{f}
.

Finally, we look at the matrix
\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*}
(for which we give two slightly different formulas):
The matrix elements are given as

\displaystyle(\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*})_{of}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s,a,s^{\prime})}\cdot h^{*}_{(s,a,s^{\prime}),f}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,a,s^{\prime})\colon h(s,a,s^{\prime})=f}\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\delta_{(s,a,s^{\prime})}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\sum_{(s,a,s^{\prime})\colon h(s,a,s^{\prime})=f}\delta_{(s,a,s^{\prime})}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\delta_{f}(h(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})).

This is the expected discounted number of times that one encounters the abstraction
f
.
Using that
\FGamma\circ h^{*}=h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}
by
Proposition

C.1
, we can also write this as

\displaystyle\big(\bp\circ h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}\big)_{of}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{T}}(\bp\circ h^{T^{*}})_{o\mathbf{f}}\cdot(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})_{\mathbf{f}f}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{T}}\big[b(o)_{h^{T}}\big](\mathbf{f})\sum_{t=0}^{T}\gamma^{t}\delta_{f}(f_{t}).

This can also be described as the expected discounted number of times that one encounters the abstraction
f
.

C.4
The resulting commutative diagram

Building on
Section

C.2
, the following is a commutative diagram of belief models and model morphisms, with four differently colored “connected components”; one can sometimes use
Proposition

C.1
in the process of showing that every linear function in the diagram is a morphism of belief models, and that the final diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\Xi}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime\mathcal{F}^{T}}_{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}^{T}}}}

For example, the following diagram visualizes the fact that
h^{*}:\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}
is a morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subseteq$}\\
\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{F}}\end{subarray}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subseteq$}\\
\im(h^{*})\end{subarray}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{O}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma\circ h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bp\circ\FGamma}

This gives rise to the following diagram of ambiguities:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\FGamma(\ker(\bp^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma\big(\ker(\bp\circ\FGamma)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\bp)\cap\im(\FGamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\bp)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\FGamma\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime})\cap\im(h^{*})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FGamma\big(\ker(\bp\circ\FGamma)\cap\im(h^{*})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\bp)\cap\im(\FGamma\circ h^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\bp)\cap\im\big(h^{T^{*}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}(\FGamma\circ h^{*})\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime}\circ h^{*})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\FGamma\circ h^{*})\big(\ker(\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}\big(\ker(\bp\circ h^{T^{*}})\cap\im(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{T^{*}}\big(\ker(\bp\circ h^{T^{*}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\FGamma\circ h^{*})\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime\prime\prime})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\big(h^{T^{*}}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}}\big)\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime\prime}\circ\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}h^{T^{*}}\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime\prime})\cap\im(\FGamma_{\mathcal{F}})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\subseteq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}h^{T^{*}}\big(\ker(\bp^{\prime\prime})\big)}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The ambiguities are computed using
Proposition

2.9
, and the inclusions and equalities of ambiguities follow from
Theorem

3.2
and
Theorem

3.5
.
Here, the ambiguity
\FGamma\big(\ker(\bp\circ\FGamma)\big)=\ker(\bp)\cap\im(\FGamma)
is the special case discussed in depth in
Lang et al. (2024)
.
Note that the models
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}}
are closely related to the models
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}_{3}
from
Section

3.3
.

Assume we would use one of these models in practice.
The further right or up it is in the diagram, the more ambiguity there is, but it is then also more likely that the model covers the true belief model (should it appear in the diagram in the first place).
Thus, there is a trade-off between covering the true belief model, and keeping the ambiguity small.

Appendix D
Details on the example with invariant features

Here, we present more mathematical details for
Section

3.3
.
This appendix is not self-contained and we recommend reading it alongside the section in the main paper.

D.1
Details on the MDP and observations

Formally, the states are given by
\mathcal{S}=(\{L,R\}\times\{U,D\})^{2}
, with the first component being the hand-position, and the second component being the button position.
For example, the state in
Equation

12
is given by
((L,U),(R,D))
.

Furthermore, we define functions
\Pos_{H}:\mathcal{S}\to\{L,R\}\times\{U,D\}
and
\Pos_{B}:\mathcal{S}\to\{L,R\}\times\{U,D\}
as the first and second projection.
These are the position of a “hand”
H
and a “button”
B
, in a 2x2 gridworld.
Then the state
s
from
Equation

12
satisfies
\Pos_{H}(s)=(L,U)
and
\Pos_{B}(s)=(R,D)
.

The set of trajectories is formally given by
\Xi=(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})^{3}\times\mathcal{S}
.
The set of observations is formally given by

\mathcal{O}=\Big[\{L,R\}^{2}\times\{P,\overline{P}\}\Big]^{3}\times\{L,R\}^{2}.

Here,
\overline{P}
means that it was
not
observed that a button was pressed.

D.2
Details on the belief models and symmetries

Let
G=D_{4}
be the dihedral group of order
8
, i.e., the symmetry group of the square.
It is given by

G=D_{4}=\left\{e,\ r,\ r^{2},\ r^{3},\ f,\ rf,\ r^{2}f,\ r^{3}f\right\},

where
r
is a clockwise rotations by
90^{\circ}
and
f
is a flip over the horizontal axis.
In compositions, we apply
f
first.

G
acts on
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
by individually acting on states and actions:

g.(s,a)\coloneqq(g.s,g.a),

where
g.s=g.(\Pos_{H}(s),\Pos_{B}(s))\coloneqq(g.\Pos_{H}(s),g.\Pos_{B}(s))
, where on the generators
g=r
and
g=f
we have

\displaystyle r.(L,U)

\displaystyle=(R,U),\quad r.(R,U)=(R,D),\quad r.(R,D)=(L,D),\quad r.(L,D)=(L,U)

\displaystyle f.(L,U)

\displaystyle=(L,D),\quad f.(R,U)=(R,D),\quad f.(R,D)=(R,U),\quad f.(L,D)=(L,U).

This specifies the action on states.
On actions, we specify

\displaystyle r.L

\displaystyle=U,\quad r.U=R,\quad r.R=D,\quad r.D=L,\quad r.P=P.

\displaystyle f.L

\displaystyle=L,\quad f.U=D,\quad f.R=R,\quad f.D=U,\quad f.P=P.

Thus, the “pressing” action remains invariant.

With this group action, we obtain a set of equivalence classes of state-action pairs, given by
\overline{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
.
A set of
representatives
for the equivalence classes is given by

\big(\{s_{0}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{s_{0}}\big)\cup\big(\{s_{1}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{s_{1}}\big)\cup\big(\{s_{2}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{s_{2}}\big),

(23)

where

s_{0}=((R,D),(R,D)),\quad s_{1}=((L,D),(R,D)),\quad s_{2}=((L,U),(R,D)),

and where the (state-dependent) set of actions are given by

\mathcal{A}^{s_{0}}=\mathcal{A}^{s_{2}}=\{L,D,P\},\quad\mathcal{A}^{s_{1}}=\{L,R,U,D,P\}.

We then have a function

h:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\bigcup_{i\in\{0,1,2\}}\{s_{i}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{s_{i}}

that maps each state-action pair to a representative, given by
h(s,a)=g.(s,a)
for the unique
g\in D_{4}
for which
g.(s,a)
is in the set of representatives from
Equation

23
.
Via
h
, we now identify
\overline{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
with
\bigcup_{i\in\{0,1,2\}}\{s_{i}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{s_{i}}
.

Equation

15
can be showed by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\xi,(s,a)}&=\big[\lambda(\xi)\big](s,a)\\
&=\sum_{t=0}^{2}\delta_{(s,a)}(h(s_{t},a_{t}))\\
&=\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\delta_{(s,a)}(h(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}))\sum_{t=0}^{2}\delta_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})}(s_{t},a_{t})\\
&=\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}h^{*}_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),(s,a)}\FGamma_{\xi,(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})}\\
&=\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\FGamma_{\xi,(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})}h^{*}_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),(s,a)}\\
&=(\FGamma\circ h^{*})_{\xi,(s,a)}.\end{split}

(24)

For the matrix elements of
h^{*}
, we used
Equation

1
.

The human’s belief
\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}(s,a\mid o)
for
(s,a)\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
and
o\in\mathcal{O}
is then given as follows:
The human has a uniform prior
B(s)=P_{0}
over possible start-states sampled from
P_{0}
, and a uniform prior over possibly next actions given the current state, leading to a prior distribution over
B(\xi)\in\Delta(\Xi)
.
Then, upon seeing
o
, the human implicitly computes a posterior belief over trajectories compatible with the observation, simply given by

B(\xi\mid o)\propto\delta_{o}(O(\xi))\cdot B(\xi).

(25)

Equation

16
can be showed by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}_{o,(s,a)}&=\big[\epsilon(o)\big](s,a)\\
&=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\big[b(o)\big](\xi)\cdot\big[\lambda(\xi)\big](s,a)\\
&=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\bp_{o\xi}\cdot\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\xi,(s,a)}\\
&=(\bp\circ\mathbf{\Lambda})_{o,(s,a)}\\
&=(\bp\circ\FGamma\circ h^{*})_{o,(s,a)}\end{split}

(26)

) and the sentence following it for computing
\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}(\overline{R})(o)
for an observation
o
.

Since we assumed that the starting state is one of the states in
Equation

13
, the human has the belief
\big[\epsilon(o_{2})\big](s_{2},P)=3
, i.e., the human is certain that, up to symmetry, the hand performed a pressing action three times in
s_{2}
.
Thus,

0=\big[\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}(\overline{R})\big](o_{2})=\big[\epsilon(o_{2})\big](s_{2},P)\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},P)=3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},P)

This implies
\overline{R}(s_{2},P)=0
.

Now, the first action could either not change anything,
or
horizontally align
H
and
B
.
An action that does not change anything is more likely (chance
2/3
since there are two actions, in the direction of two different adjacent walls, that achieve this, which both correspond to action
L
up to symmetry), and so we obtain

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{1})\big](s_{2},L)=2/3,\quad

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{1})\big](s_{2},P)=4/3,

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{1})\big](s_{2},D)=1/3,\quad

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{1})\big](s_{1},P)=2/3.

Compare also with (
26
).
Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}(\overline{R})\big](o_{1})

\displaystyle=2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},L)+4/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},P)+1/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},D)+2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{1},P)

\displaystyle=2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{1},P).

Here, we used that
\overline{R}(s_{2},P)=0
by what we showed before, and
\overline{R}(s_{2},L)=\overline{R}(s_{2},D)=0
since
\overline{R}(s,a)=0
whenever
a\neq P
(i.e., since
\overline{R}\in\mathcal{V}
).
Thus, we have
\overline{R}(s_{1},P)=0
as well.

o_{0}

Again, there is a chance of
2/3
that the first action does not change anything.
Given the first step, everything which follows is deterministic, leading to these feature beliefs:

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{2},L)=2/3,\quad

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{2},R)=2/3,\quad\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{1},P)=2/3

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{2},D)=1/3,\quad

\displaystyle\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{1},R)=1/3,\quad\big[\epsilon(o_{0})\big](s_{0},P)=1/3.

Compare again with (
26
).
This means that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}(\overline{R})\big](o_{0})

\displaystyle=2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},L)+2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},R)+2/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{1},P)+

\displaystyle\ \ \ +1/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{2},D)+1/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{1},R)+1/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{0},P)

\displaystyle=1/3\cdot\overline{R}(s_{0},P).

Here, we used that
\overline{R}(s_{1},P)
by what we showed before, together, again, with the fact that
\overline{R}(s,a)=0
for all
a\neq P
.
That shows
\overline{R}(s_{0},P)=0
.

D.4
Details on the ambiguity analysis for
\mathcal{M}_{3}

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big[(\bp\circ\FGamma)(R^{\prime})\big](o)&=(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s_{1}^{\prime},P)}\cdot R^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime},P)+(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},P)}\cdot R^{\prime}(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},P)\\
&=(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s_{1}^{\prime},P)}-(\bp\circ\FGamma)_{o,(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},P)}\\
&=0\end{split}

(27)

In the computation, the second step follows from the definition of
R^{\prime}
.
The last step follows from the symmetry remarked on before.

Appendix E
Mathematical interpretations of related work in our framework

In this appendix, we briefly interpret some of the related work from
Section

4.2
in our framework for the special case that they learn linear reward probes.
Note that these interpretations are not meant to capture everything there is to say about that work — the summaries we provide are quite coarse.
In all examples below, we assume access to a very capable foundation model
\widehat{\lambda}:\Xi\to\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}
that allows for a linear ontology translation
\Psi:\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}\to\mathds{R}^{\Omega}
to the human’s ontology
\lambda
, as in
Section

3.4.1
:
\Psi\circ\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda
.
Define
\Phi\coloneqq\Psi^{T}:\mathds{R}^{\Omega}\to\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}
, which then satisfies
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\circ\Phi=\mathbf{\Lambda}
by
Proposition

A.2
.
In all approaches below, we define
\widehat{\epsilon}
and assume that the return function is learned with the same method as in
Section

3.4.2
.
Notably, in all of the approaches one essentially just defines
\widehat{\epsilon}\coloneqq\widehat{\lambda}
, i.e., no explicit modeling of humans is performed.

E.1
Amplified oversight and eliciting latent knowledge

In amplified oversight, one
amplifies
the human to give accurate feedback, which means we can assume
\mathcal{O}=\Xi
and
\epsilon=\lambda
.
One approach to achieve this would be to essentially
define

\epsilon\coloneqq\Psi\circ\widehat{\lambda}
by giving the human access to the linear ontology translation
\Psi
for understanding the foundation model’s thoughts.
This would roughly be in the spirit of eliciting latent knowledge
(
Christiano et al., 2021
)
, where the human can query a reporter to give information about arbitrary latent knowledge of an AI.

Accordingly, one can also choose
\widehat{\epsilon}=\widehat{\lambda}
, leading to the following coverage diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}

The ontologies and feature belief functions are then the same, which automatically means that the ambiguity disappears:
\mathbf{\Lambda}\big(\ker(\mathbf{\Lambda})\big)=0
.

E.2
Easy-to-hard generalization

In this setting,
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\Xi
is a
subset
of trajectories that the human correctly understands.
Thus, for
\xi\in\mathcal{O}
, one has
\epsilon(\xi)=\lambda(\xi)
, and so
\epsilon=\lambda|_{\mathcal{O}}
is simply a restriction.
In this setting, one can also set
\widehat{\epsilon}=\widehat{\lambda}|_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Now, let
\mathbf{\Lambda}|
and
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}|
be the linear functions corresponding to
\lambda|_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}|_{\mathcal{O}}
, respectively, via
Proposition

A.1
.
One obtains the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\mathcal{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Lambda}|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}|}

For
\Phi
in this diagram to be a morphism, we need that the lower diagram commutes.
With
Proposition

A.2
, this follows from the assumption that
\Phi^{T}=\Psi
is an ontology translation:

\Psi\circ\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda
implies
\Psi\circ\widehat{\lambda}|_{\mathcal{O}}=\lambda|_{\mathcal{O}}
.
The ambiguity is now given by
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\big(\ker(\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}|)\big)
.
By using
Theorem

2.13
, this ambiguity disappears if and only if for all
\xi\in\Xi
, we have
\widehat{\lambda}(\xi)\in\Big\{\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}Z_{\xi}\widehat{\lambda}(\xi)\mid Z_{\xi}\in\mathds{R}\Big\}
.
Thus, the ambiguity vanishes if the trajectories that the human understands have enough variety in the vector space of feature strengths.

E.3
Classical RLHF and weak-to-strong generalization

In classical RLHF, without any safeguards, one just uses the model
\widehat{\epsilon}=\widehat{\lambda}
as the feature belief function even though
\epsilon\neq\lambda
and hopes for the best:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\widehat{\Omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}^{\Xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}

(28)

In this case, the lower triangle does not commute since
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\circ\Phi\neq\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}
, which, using
Proposition

A.2
, is due to
\Psi\circ\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda\neq\epsilon
.
This means that the second model does not cover the true belief model, and so the guarantee from
Theorem

3.2
breaks.
In fact, the return function that would be inferred using this model is
G_{\mathcal{O}}
, the observation return function itself (cf. the definition of feedback-compatible return functions,
Definition

2.8
, applied to this faulty model).

Lang et al. (2024)
extensively discuss failure modes in this case, called deceptive inflation and overjustification.
We note that weak-to-strong generalization
(
Burns et al., 2023a
)
, when used without additional techniques, also considers this setting, but tries to ensure that the learning process or
\widehat{\lambda}
contains inductive biases that steer the learning process to learn
G
anyway.
